TWO TIERS. Each lane leads with its top 12 stories in full - headline, outlet, timestamp, summary, link - and then ends with an 'ALSO IN <LANE>' list of further stories as bare headlines. Demoted is not rejected: those cleared the same date filter and merely ranked lower on cross-outlet pickup, which is a weak signal. Read them. If one matters, use it - but you have only the headline, so say only what the headline supports and invent nothing to pad it out. Full-text items are never demoted.

LANE NAMES DESCRIBE THE FEED, NOT THE TOPIC. SCMP sits in East Asia but carries US politics; Al Jazeera sits in Middle East but carries US domestic stories. Never infer a story's subject from the lane it appears under.

A bill that has already drawn significant progressive Democratic support is the Block the Bombs Act , which would forbid the transfer to Israel of some of the most destructive bombs in the U.S. arsenal, such as so-called bunker-buster bombs. The legislation has attracted more than 80 co-sponsors, and while it has no chance of passing the House this year, it is seen as a good candidate for being reintroduced next year with even more momentum behind it. “The Block the Bombs Act has been a tremendous organizing vehicle that went further than many of us even thought,” said Hassan El-Tayyab, the legislative director for Midd

Hamas’s assault on Israel in 2023 was said to be ushering in a new Middle East. The U.S. attacks on Iran and the fight over the Strait of Hormuz are said to be bringing yet another one into being. Per U.S. President Donald Trump , “The Middle East will be transformed, and the future of that region has never—I don’t think it’s ever looked brighter.” Foreign Affairs weighed in with an article titled “The Iran Imperative: How America and Israel Can Shape a New Middle East.” Bloody and chaotic events like the Iran war induce fantasies of comprehensive change, but reality keeps coming up short. As Sam Rayburn, House speaker in an earlier era observed: “Any jackass can kick down a barn, but it takes a carpenter to build one.” Decisive change for the better—in the United States’ conception—demands an actual preponderance of U.S. military power and the ability to convert that power into enduring political arrangements. The United States has imagined itself as wielding both attributes while possessing neither. The use of force halfway around the world requires political leaders to show that the stakes are commensurate with the cost and risk. That means war aims are framed in extravagant terms that are eventually revealed as detached from quotidian reality. On the other side, adversaries have agency. In a few months, Europe will mark a date of great moment. The Treaty of Rome, signed in March 1957, established what came to be the European Union. The 70th anniversary celebrations will, however, be relatively low-key. Italian Prime Minister Giorgia Meloni has ensured that the biannual meeting of the European Council take place in the Italian capital. There will be tributes, official speeches, but—at the start of another likely turbulent year for the continent—few governments seem interested in hailing an organization that has come to be denounced by some as elitist and out of touch. In immediate political terms, their reluctance might be understandable. The EU has struggled to understand, let alone address, the roots of populism; it has struggled to get its voice heard in the big global conflagrations, from Israel-Palestine to Iran to the Trump administration’s curious love-in with Russian President Vladimir Putin. And it has struggled to find a language that communicates with voters, particularly younger ones. But symbolic moments such as this provide opportunities for a refresh, just as the results of the U.S. midterm elections appear to point to a liberal world that is belatedly finding a way of standing up to the far right. Yet, amid all the talk of a “blue tsunami” and the jubilation at the Democrats’ stunning seizure of both houses of Congress, quiet heads are counseling that it is far too early to write off the nationalist extremists who have held politics around the world in a vice-like grip for so long. It is possible that Donald Trump might temper his behavior—but then again, he might not. He might instead use his remaining two years in office to lash out. His mind does not work in rational or predictable ways. And in any case might the MAGA movement find another, more professional, less mercurial standard-bearer? The iss
  https://foreignpolicy.com/2026/11/10/democrats-us-europe-eu-center-left/

* The Global Economy Braces for Yet Another Blow
  Foreign Policy | 10 Nov 11:00 UTC
  El Niño is already unleashing economic and agricultural pain worldwide. It may get even worse.
  FULL TEXT: Get audio access with any FP subscription. Deadly flooding sweeping the American Southwest . Unrelenting downpours clobbering South America . A punishing drought gripping Southeast Asia . The world is still seeing the evolution of El Niño, the naturally occurring phenomenon that scrambles weather patterns worldwide and can wreak havoc on agricultural production. But these snapshots offer a window—and warning—for what could come as the full force of this powerful event crashes into a global economy that is already battered by the Iran war and months of trade turmoil. Deadly flooding sweeping the American Southwest . Unrelenting downpours clobbering South America . A punishing drought gripping Southeast Asia . The world is still seeing the evolution of El Niño, the naturally occurring phenomenon that scrambles weather patterns worldwide and can wreak havoc on agricultural production. But these snapshots offer a window—and warning—for what could come as the full force of this powerful event crashes into a global economy that is already battered by the Iran war and months of trade turmoil. “The effects of these El Niños are not benign,” said Cullen Hendrix, a senior fellow at the Peterson Institute for International Economics who has estimated that this event could cost the global economy more than $7 trillion over the next five years. “We’re talking about a phenomenon that has systemic impacts that can adversely affect populations all across the globe.” The world is no stranger to this phenomenon, which occurs about every two to seven years and can last for up to 18 months . But no two El Niños are identical . “Each one has its own little fingerprint,” said Brian Barker, a principal faculty specialist at the University of Maryland who works for NASA Harvest, the agency’s food security and agriculture program. “Until we actually see it develop and come to fruition, we won’t know exactly what it’s going to be.” And this year’s event is shaping into what could be the strongest El Niño ever recorded . The U.S. National Oceanic and Atmospheric Administration has forecast an 83 percent likelihood that the world sees a strong-to-very strong El Niño in early 2027, and the World Meteorological Organization (WMO) has projected that the strength of this event could exceed the scale of its global monitoring charts. Governments, banks , and humanitarian agencies are bracing for substantial disruptions to critical infrastructure, food production, and global inflation . Imp
  https://foreignpolicy.com/2026/11/10/el-nino-economy-agriculture-cost/

* Here’s why last year’s government shutdown will haunt October’s inflation data.
  NYT Economy | 10 Nov 10:48 UTC
  FULL TEXT: U.S. inflation remained elevated last month, underscoring the need for the Federal Reserve to raise interest rates again this year. Overall prices rose 0.4 percent in October after a 0.6 percent jump in September, according to the latest Consumer Price Index report from the Bureau of Labor Statistics. “Core” prices, which strip out volatile food and energy items, rose 0.3 percent. The agency on Tuesday did not release year-over-year figures for October because it was unable to collect data during last year’s government shutdown. According to calculations by The New York Times, overall prices were around 4 percent higher compared with the same time last year, up from 3.7 percent in September. Over the same period, core prices rose about 2.8 percent. One of the biggest drivers of inflation in October was food. Prices rose 0.5 percent last month and about 3 percent since the same time last year. That likely reflects a surge in energy prices, especially diesel prices , which have exploded costs for farmers. Energy prices overall rose modestly in October and were up 0.7 percent for the month after a 5.2 percent jump in September. Gasoline prices rose 1 percent, significantly slower than the 9.1 percent increase during the previous period. Electricity prices rose nearly 1 percent. October’s C.P.I. report was the last before the Fed meets for its final gathering of the year on Dec. 8-9. Policymakers have signaled that they will raise rates next month as they seek to stamp out price pressures that have pushed inflation further from their goal. While the Fed tracks C.P.I., it officially targets 2 percent inflation, as measured by the Personal Consumption Expenditures index. That level has been overshot for nearly six years. Fed officials also watch the price of services as an indicator of the underlying pace of inflation. Specifically, they have been looking at core services excluding housing, a measure sometimes referred to as “supercore.” Those prices rose 0.4 percent in October, and have risen at an annual rate of nearly 5.5 percent over the past three months. On Tuesday, Christopher J. Waller, a Fed governor, raised the prospects of a larger-than-usual half-point interest rate increase at the bank’s meeting in December if inflation did not show signs of moderating. In remarks delivered at a Bank of England event, Mr. Waller said a quarter-point increase would suffice if price pressures improved. But if “core” inflation rose 0.3 percent or more, as October’s dat
  https://www.nytimes.com/live/2026/11/10/business/inflation-cpi-report-october/heres-why-last-years-government-shutdown-will-haunt-octobers-inflation-data

* Analysis: Which countries have sent the most delegates to COP31?
  Carbon Brief | 10 Nov 17:21 UTC
  More than 92,000 people have registered to attend the COP31 climate summit in Antalya, Turkey, potentially making it the largest… The post Analysis: Which countries have sent the most delegates to COP31? appeared first on Carbon Brief .
  FULL TEXT: More than 92,000 people have registered to attend the COP31 climate summit in Antalya, Turkey, potentially making it the largest COP in history. Carbon Brief analysis of the provisional lists of delegates, published by the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC), reveals that more than 14,000 delegates have been registered as “host country guests”. For comparison, the provisional list for COP30 in Belém, Brazil, last year included only around 1,000 delegates in this category. As with COP30 , Donald Trump’s US government has not sent a delegation. Other no-shows include Israel and Myanmar. It was reported ahead of the summit that Taliban officials from Afghanistan had been registered for the event as observers; however, there are no delegates from observer states in the provisional lists. With more than 3,000 “virtual” delegates signed up to attend the summit online, the total number of people registered for the event surpasses 95,000. Further analysis of the participant lists will be added to this article in due course. With 92,934 participants registered, COP31 is provisionally the largest COP in history – topping COP28 in Dubai , which was attended by more than 80,000 people. This total is based on the delegates that have registered to be at the summit in person. The UNFCCC releases the final figures – based on participants collecting a physical badge at the venue – after the summit has ended. The chart below shows how the provisional figures for COP31 compare to the final totals in previous COPs – going back to COP1 in Berlin in 1995. The participant lists provided by the UNFCCC are divided between the different types of groups and organisations attending the summit. The largest group at COP31 is for delegates representing parties. These are nation-states, plus the European Union, that have ratified the convention and play a full part in negotiations. This group adds up to 18,812 delegates – the second largest after COP28. (In keeping with recent COPs, the UNFCCC has published spreadsheets that name every single person that has registered for the summit – excluding support staff. Previously, COPs have typically included thousands of “overflow” participants in which countries and UN agencies could nominate delegates without their names appearing on their official lists.) For consistency with Carbon Brief’s analysis of previous COPs , the above chart includes overflow delegates as a single group. However, the participant lists do di
  https://www.carbonbrief.org/analysis-which-countries-have-sent-the-most-delegates-to-cop31

  "Fraudsters have long counterfeited high-end wine. Now they are coming for the bottles on your supermarket shelves."
  FULL TEXT: While wine fraud has been around for nearly 150 years, it exploded around the turn of the century, with enterprising counterfeiters drawn by high margins and relatively low risk. Now, though, with the highest-end manufacturers upping their game, would-be forgers are looking at more moderately priced targets; they’ve even come for mass-market brands like Yellow Tail. For 1843 , William Ralston uncorks the zero-somm game. Other popular anti-counterfeiting techniques used by high-end wineries include manufacturing proprietary bottle shapes and micro-printing, where text and patterns are printed at a scale visible only under magnification. (I was shown a bottle of 2009 Le Pin, a fine Bordeaux wine, where the names of the winery owner’s children had been micro-printed repeatedly between two thin red lines on its front label.) Some wineries have adopted advanced security measures, incorporating features like holograms, individualised digital tracking codes and inks that change colour when tilted. Pétrus, a Bordeaux winery which produces only 30,000 bottles a year, uses inks custom-made by the same company that produces the ones used on American dollar bills. “How Jos Leijdekkers turned Sierra Leone into a drug-dealer’s paradise.” “The plant-based boom failed to deliver. She couldn’t speak, so she made writing motions until someone put a pen in her hand, and – stoned, half-conscious – she wrote glottis . Wendy Lau understood. They fixed it. Then came what she called the worst 12 days of her life. The valve replacement was failing. The post Can India’s Oldest Political Dynasty Find a New Mandate? ap...
  FULL TEXT: On Sept. 7, 2022, Rahul Gandhi stepped out for a walk. What followed was a journey of 2,500 miles over 145 days, starting from Kanyakumari, the southernmost tip of India, and traversing the whole country. For weeks, he walked through villages, towns and cities, listening to farmers, students, workers and activists along the way. In a country where leaders traditionally address carefully curated crowds from a heavily barricaded stage, this was groundbreaking. Here was a man, born into India’s most recognizable political family, talking to them without any political theater. This was evident on the evening of Oct. 2, the birth anniversary of Mahatma Gandhi (no relation to Rahul Gandhi), when the Bharat Jodo Yatra (Unite India March) rally through Mysuru in Karnataka was marred by rain. And yet, as water poured down, Rahul addressed the crowd, his voice booming over the chaos. The image of a drenched leader addressing an equally drenched crowd stayed on the phones of his supporters for months. Four days later, in Mandya, his mother, Sonia Gandhi, joined the march. When Rahul noticed that her shoelace had come undone, he bent down to tie it himself, creating a tender moment that quickly went viral. During a second march, the Bharat Jodo Nyay Yatra, as Bharatiya Janata Party (BJP) workers in Madhya Pradesh greeted him with chants of “Modi, Modi” and “Jai Shri Ram” (Hail Lord Rama), attempting to needle him, Rahul got down from his vehicle and shook hands with them. The march helped to retire Rahul’s image as a part-time politician who appeared before elections and vanished afterward, replacing it with that of a man willing to spend months on the road, listening before speaking.   FULL TEXT: Twenty-two years since their emergence as a rebel group in Yemen, the Houthis have become arguably the most powerful nonstate actor in the world. How and why did they manage such a feat? There is no doubt that Iran was significant in empowering and strengthening the movement and its military capabilities. Yet Iranian support, and the Yemeni army arsenal they seized, are not the only factors behind the Houthis’ strength. Their enemies’ strategic mistakes, miscalculations, and absence of coherent strategies and policies — along with incompetence, inefficiency and divisions among local rivals — also boosted and emboldened them. There was a strategic myopia among all actors — local, regional and international. The Houthis are often described as lucky to have had such irrational and fragmented enemies. Even Yemen’s rugged terrain worked to their advantage. And in the end, they emerged as such a powerful force that their ambitions are no longer confined by geographic boundaries. In 2004, a group of fighters in the mountainous terrain of Saada governorate, bordering Saudi Arabia, began challenging the authority of the government of former President Ali Abdullah Saleh. The group gradually transformed from al-Shabab al-Mumin (“The Faithful Youth”), as they were first known in the 1990s, into Ansar Allah, better known as the Houthi movement, led by Hussein al-Houthi. They began chanting slogans similar to those of the Islamic Republic of Iran, such as “Death to America” and “Death to Israel.” Their behavior angered Saleh. At that time, few were even aware of the Houthis’ existence, though in small numbers they were chanting these slogans as early as 2002, according to some reports. Hussein al-Houthi was a member of Parliament between 1993 and 1997. Saleh considered him and his supporters a challenge to his authority in Saada. Mediation efforts aimed at convincing them to hand themselves over to the police were unsuccessful, and clashes erupted on June 18, 2004, as government forces attempted to arrest the Houthi leader. Mediation continued even after the clashes, but al-Houthi refused to surrender. On Sept. 10, the government announced the killing of the rebel leader and the end of the insurgency. Yet the latter would survive, even outlasting Saleh’s regime. * Equality talk
  Aeon | 09 Nov 11:00 UTC
  In the summer of 1646, the idea of equality gained new and profound depths. We are still catching up with the consequences - by Teresa M Bejan Read on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: Striking dockworkers, England, 1954. Photo by Marc Riboud/Magnum Photos It’s no accident that equality comes first among the ‘self-evident’ truths articulated in the United States Declaration of Independence. The claim that ‘all men are created equal’ serves there not simply as an abstract premise from which revolutionary consequences ought to follow, but as a concrete practical foundation on which the American colonists could claim to stand as equals with the greatest king and hold him to account. Still impressed by his own handiwork 50 years later, Thomas Jefferson even prophesied that the ‘palpable truth’ of equality enshrined in the Declaration would one day unite all of humankind. Yet today, it’s evident that the word ‘equality’ means very different things to different people. What once seemed capable of providing common ground is now just another source of controversy. What explains this shift? I suspect that the deceptively simple, conceptual language of ‘equality’ has itself played a substantial role. A better understanding of ‘equality talk’ – as the application of this language to human beings and their relations – might therefore shed some light on our predicament. L et’s begin by examining a group of people that generally prides itself on thinking (and speaking) more clearly than most: professional philosophers. What are they doing when they talk about equality? Often, they’re saying something about human beings: about what we’re fundamentally like, and how we should be treated morally, politically and socially. When an academic philosopher says that ‘all people are equal’, she’s generally saying that, despite differences of race and gender, economic class or individual merit, every person is somehow the same in some fundamental respect(s). While some philosophers will stress the human capacity for reason and moral agency, others emphasise our mutual vulnerability to suffering and violent death. Still, all generally agree that this sameness – whatever its underlying basis – ought to matter for our social and political arrangements, such that any persistent inequalities between human beings must require some justification or redress. But here, a non-philosopher might wonder why the professionals insist on using the technical language of ‘equality’ as opposed to something more straightforward like ‘sameness’? One reason, I think, is that they are trading on the former concept’s mathematical resonances of quantitative precision and transcendent tr
  https://aeon.co/essays/historical-ideas-of-equality-help-us-imagine-an-equal-world

* The Poppy’s Many Meanings
  New Lines | 06 Nov 11:42 UTC
  The flower is used to commemorate sacrifice in war, but the humble weed has far more negative connotations in some countries. The post The Poppy’s Many Meanings appeared first on New Lines Magazine .
  FULL TEXT: A planted seed is generally left well alone to sprout and grow in the darkness, perhaps with the help of water or fertilizer. But there is a notable exception: Poppies require light to germinate, meaning the earth must be disturbed before they grow. This feature of the flower explains much about its ubiquity and patterns across landscapes. It appears in particular density in places where earth has been turned over, such as farmland or cemeteries — or where trenches have been dug to protect troops in war. Nov. 11 is Remembrance Day across the Commonwealth, with poppies seen on lapels throughout the month, and memorial events held on the Sunday closest to the day itself — this year, this coming Sunday. These spots of color on coats symbolize remembrance of, and gratitude to, those who lost their lives fighting in war, drawing on images from World War I of the poppies that sprouted, undeterred by violence, all along the front lines of Europe. These front lines shifted regularly, often by tiny amounts, but each time requiring more trenches to be dug and more soil to be disrupted — so that more poppies grew. Poppies on battlefields had been noted during the Napoleonic Wars of the early 19th century, but the image was cemented in the minds of generations of schoolchildren by a poem scribbled in the back of an ambulance during World War I, by Canadian physician John McCrae. It begins: “In Flanders fields, the poppies blow / Between the crosses, row on row / That mark our place.” Its patriotism, signaling the rightness of dying for one’s country, is suitable for the military parades of Remembrance Sunday, and the simplicity and the color of the poppy make it an ideal emblem, adopted in 1921 by the newly formed Royal British Legion to fundraise to help those who served in the war. Neither the poem nor the poppy are uncontroversial, however. “Take up our quarrel with the foe!” the last verse begins, signaling a commitment to conflict many find abhorrent; these people shun the poppy or instead choose to wear a white version, symbolizing peace rather than war. But there are far wider problems, worldwide, with what poppies represent. From the opium wars in China to the poppy fields of India and Afghanistan to the opioid crisis in America, the poppy has been a source of addiction, conflict and misery — and, for a few, wealth. And these dichotomies, between wealth and grinding poverty, memories of noble sacrifice or of oppression and addiction, are not the only meanings 
  https://newlinesmag.com/essays/the-poppys-many-meanings/

* Celestial nuisance
  Aeon | 06 Nov 11:00 UTC
  The cosmos has become a lab for the Big Questions in physics. Along the way, we’ve lost sight of its glorious messiness - by Dylan Jow Read on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: Journey to a satellite galaxy roughly 200,000 light-years from Earth, featuring the young star cluster NGC 602. With data from Webb’s NIRCam ( Near-InfraRed Camera ) and MIRI ( Mid-InfraRed Instrument ). Courtesy HUBBLE/WEBB/ESA In the story of cosmic history, one of the most important supporting characters is dust. As clouds of interstellar gas cooled and condensed in the early Universe, dust grains absorbed and re-emitted radiation, eventually forming stars. Later, dust provided the raw material from which planets could form and grow. Dust grains may even have been the surfaces on which the first complex organic molecules formed. Yet, if you ask most cosmologists, they’ll tell you that dust is a sheer nuisance. In astrophysics, ‘nuisance’ is a quasi-technical term. Astrophysicists call any phenomenon that gets in the way of the thing they actually want to study a ‘nuisance’. Dust grains emit polarised light that can mimic subtle signals from the afterglow of the Big Bang (the cosmic microwave background) that cosmologists have spent decades and billions of dollars trying to detect. A cosmologist studies dust not to uncover its role in shaping the cosmos, but to subtract it – the term ‘sweeping under the rug’ couldn’t be more apt. This dichotomy between ‘signal’ and ‘nuisance’ is found across many sub-fields in astronomy. Astronomers who study a phenomenon called ‘weak lensing’ use the subtle distortions of light caused by gravity to map invisible dark matter. For them, the ordinary matter that makes up everything we can see is a nuisance. Pulsar timing astronomers use rapidly spinning neutron stars called pulsars as extraordinarily precise clocks to detect the passage of gravitational waves through the galaxy. For them, the nuisance is ionised interstellar gas, which scatters and disperses the light from pulsars. Yet one astronomer’s nuisance is another’s treasure. Just as a laser beam reveals the intricate swirls of smoke in a dark room, pulsars illuminate the fine structure of the interstellar medium, the unlit space between galaxies. If you want to study the interstellar medium and its role in shaping our galaxy’s evolution, the light-scattering gas is an invaluable resource. The ESO-operated Very Large Telescope based at the Cerro Paranal site in the Atacama Desert of northern Chile. Photo by Miguel Claro/ESO Whether a phenomenon is a nuisance or something worth studying in its own right clearly depends on the investigator and their interests. But in
  https://aeon.co/essays/why-astrophysics-needs-to-abandon-the-big-questions

The fix O’Gorman endorses may sound drastic, but his precautionary principle is shared by invasion biology, the subdiscipline devoted to the study of species transplanted from their native ranges. There is a long history of organisms moved by humans, whether deliberately or inadvertently, wreaking total havoc upon their new homes. The brown tree snake, native to Indonesia, northern Australia, and Papua New Guinea, was accidentally introduced to Guam by the US military following World War II and promptly ate its way through ten of the island’s twelve endemic forest bird species. Similarly, Nile perch released into Lake Victoria by British colonial forces gulped down roughly two hundred species of homegrown cichlids, fish found nowhere else on Earth. Cane toads in Australia, Miconia trees on Hawaii and Tahiti, rats just about everywhere—the list goes on. And if biodiversity loss, however regrettable, seems like an abstraction in the scheme of things, know that human culture frequently suffers direct impoverishments because of invasives. Chestnut blight, discovered in the United States in 1904 after chestnut trees were imported from Japan, effectively deleted the American chestnut tree from the continent; its products were central to the diet and lifestyles of various indigenous peoples and communities across Appalachia. Phylloxera, an aphid relative, was imported to Europe on American grapevines during the second half of the nineteenth century. The subsequent infestation resulted in the destruction, by some estimates, of 90 percent of all European vineyards. “Godwits make the longest non-stop migration in the bird world. That might be the least of their marvels. On Oct. 27, the Iran-backed group stated that they consider Cairo’s decision to aid Riyadh a “declaration of war.” The statement came a day after Egypt’s Parliament approved Sisi’s request to provide military support to Saudi Arabia if called upon to do so. Ahmed Aboudouh, an associate fellow of the Middle East and North Africa Program at Chatham House, believes that Egypt is preparing to proactively manage the risks. He told New Lines that the operations were likely to be defensive, but that this could mean not only defending from within Saudi territory, but also defending Egypt by attempting to limit the Houthis’ ability to attack Egyptian targets. “My sense is that Egypt’s role will be confined to air and naval defense,” Aboudouh added. He explained that Saudi Arabia’s vast size and critical assets make it difficult to intercept the barrage of Houthi airstrikes — a challenge that Egypt could help with. On Oct. 10, the Houthis hit Riyadh International Airport , killing 13 people and injuring more than 300. It was the deadliest attack on the kingdom in more than a decade. In addition, Aboudouh expects Egyptian naval vessels to enter the Bab al-Mandab Strait to protect the strategic route from Houthi attacks. Ashley Stimpson | Longreads | November 5, 2026 | 18 minutes (4,985 words) In the months leading up to the nation’s bicentennial, the rickety wooden wheels of covered wagons began to roll in every corner of the United States—from the evergreen forests of Washington and windswept prairies of North Dakota to the granite peaks of Maine and palm tree-lined suburbs of Central Florida. Over the next year, the Bicentennial Wagon Train (BWT) would commemorate the founding of the country by performing the reverse of Westward Expansion, as five caravans—eventually made up of as many as 300 horse-drawn wagons—snaked their way toward Valley Forge, where they would arrive on July 4, 1976, just in time to celebrate America’s 200th birthday. Along the way, the wagon trains would stop in hundreds of cities and towns, where residents could watch teams of wagoneers construct historically accurate encampments and perform a musical revue. If so moved, citizens could also sign a so-called rededication scroll, to recommit themselves to building a more perfect union. “To commemorate this nation’s Bicentennial, we hereby dedicate ourselves anew to the precepts of our Founding Fathers,” each signer promised, inking their name on a sheath of scratchy parchment paper. Large community dinners and solemn ceremonies were planned around these vows to democracy; in some cities, whole elementary schools and entire churches lined up to sign. To accommodate those not on the wagons’ path, scroll-signing centers were set up in Holiday Inns from coast to coast. The scrolls were then loaded onto the Mayflower moving van that accompanied each train and carried toward Pennsylvania, where organizers planned to have them microfilmed and placed in an aluminum time capsule interred in the Pennsylvania dirt for the next 100 years. In 2076, organizers of the Bicentennial Wagon Train imagined, future Americans would unearth these scrolls and feel a deep sense of appreciation and admiration for the Americans of the past. Altogether, 22 million people (a tenth of the population at the time) signed approximately 917,000 scrolls, a stack of paper as high as a 20-story building . By all accounts these scrolls really did end up in Pennsylvania, where President Gerald Ford signed one, right before

  In 1975, MIT took the Shah of Iran’s money to train the country’s first generation of nuclear engineers, and the campus debate that followed, preserved in the university’s archives, anticipated the war being fought over that program today. The post How MIT Trained the Shah’s Nuclear Engineers appeared first on New Lines Magazine .
  FULL TEXT: On June 6, 1974, Jerome Wiesner booked into room 1537 at the Royal Tehran Hilton in preparation for his audience with the Shah of Iran. The electrical engineer had just celebrated his 59th birthday. In his six decades, he’d been chief scientific adviser to JFK, a name on Richard Nixon’s list of political enemies, and was now the 13th president of the Massachusetts Institute of Technology (MIT). While his friends and family had mulled over what to gift him on his birthday, Wiesner was occupied by his own deliberations over what to gift the shah. “How about one of the crystals?” a colleague posited in a letter, referring to those grown aboard NASA’s first space station. “Two of them are said to be the most perfect crystals ever grown – because of the zero g environment, of course.” The MIT professors in possession of the perfect crystals might not be willing to part with an entire specimen, he admitted. “But I should think you might be able to persuade them to donate at least a millimeter slice.” Convinced, Wiesner arranged for a sliver of the space gem to be mounted on a velvet cushion. He was already in Tehran when an angry NASA official called MIT and mumbled about the upset at the Space Center. MIT was informed that “upper levels of NASA have ‘hit the ceiling.’” Turned out it wasn’t clear who actually owned the crystals, and Wiesner’s history as a thorn in NASA’s side didn’t help matters much. Mohammad Reza Pahlavi never received the perfect space crystal (gifted instead to President Gerald Ford a few months later), but Wiesner gave him something else at that meeting in Tehran that would end up being far more useful and far more polarizing. In 1975, MIT agreed to train two cohorts of 27 Iranian nuclear scientists in a three-year graduate program that would provide them with the skills necessary to run the reactors in Iran’s fledgling nuclear industry. The students were handpicked by the shah’s new atomic energy chief and had all expenses paid. At MIT, the special program caused an uproar that eventually receded into history, packed into numbered boxes in the university’s archives. The 17-minute video, posted on Oct. 27 under the title “The People of Yemen Have Come to You,” was not just another of the critiques Fidaa has long directed at the Houthis. To some followers, it read as an attempt to dismantle a narrative years in the making by comparing the group’s words with its deeds, and by exposing a recurring pattern in which only the labels the Houthis pin on their opponents change. The video poses this central question: Can the Houthis be right in one position and then become wrongdoers in others? Does championing a just cause entitle them to override the injustices they commit inside Yemen? My research suggests the clip spread further than perhaps any video Fidaa has previously posted across his social media accounts. It circulated on a large scale, carried by his considerable digital following: some 1.8 million people on Instagram, 1.24 million on YouTube, 2.4 million on TikTok and 2.2 million on Facebook, plus about 224,000 on X. On X alone, the video had passed 11 million views at the time of writing, with tens of thousands of likes and reposts. Nor was its reach confined to the platforms where Fidaa posted it. It was shared and reposted widely on WhatsApp and Telegram; his name dominated discussion on X and Facebook; and users translated the clip into several languages, including English, French and Spanish, bringing it to a much wider audience. Fidaa’s fame did not come from nowhere. His way of presenting information and popularizing educational, moral and religious subjects has won him a wide audience, especially among young people. Alongside his digital activity, he has an academic background in control and automation engineering and holds a master’s degree from the Technical University of Kaiserslautern in Germany. His criticism of the Houthis carries added weight because of his popularity across the Arab world, which his support for Gaza has strengthened. Gaza is the cause that most shaped the Houthis’ image in the region, and it is hard for them to question his position on Palestine or accuse him of siding with Israel. * Like aliens
  Aeon | 04 Nov 11:01 UTC
  Like living metaphors, these self-contained miniature worlds show us the wondrous and fragile beauty of our biosphere - by Aeon Video Watch on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: For his ‘Biosphere Project’, the Argentinian artist and engineer Joaquín Fargas isolates ‘natural ecosystems in completely sealed containers where the only interaction with the environment is through heat and light’. These small, self-contained worlds are meant to comment on the fragility and deep interconnectedness of life on our own planet. The project reflects Fargas’s enduring commitment to creating engaging, accessible art that speaks to the urgency of the climate crisis. In his portrait of the ‘Biosphere Project’, the Argentinian filmmaker Nico Muñoz captures Fargas’s process with his own artistry, melding inventive camera angles and patient observation with archival footage from space missions and the dreamy piano of Claude Debussy’s ‘Clair de Lune’. * What It Takes to Unfence the West
  Longreads | 04 Nov 10:55 UTC
  "Can virtual fencing transform ranching in the region?"
  FULL TEXT: Barbed and wire fencing covers hundreds of thousands of miles of the American West. It is highly effective, and can be unsparing. It is also costly—”$15,000 per mile or more to replace,” Kylie Mohr writes, a devastating expense for any rancher who may’ve lost miles of boundary during a wildfire. Mohr’s feature on the dawn of virtual fencing is a stimulating history of manmade borders and the inexpert ways in which they are deployed; it’s also an optimistic bit of technology reporting on an innovation that may prove wildly beneficial for ranchers and their herds. About 620,000 miles of barbed wire and other fencing crisscrosses the West like lattices on a pie crust of an imprecise baker, dividing land and life. These fences enable people to dictate how public and private lands are grazed and how, or even if, migrating wildlife reaches its final destination. They are sometimes more trouble than they are worth, though. Just ask the ranchers who build and repair them. “Real fence is bullshit,” Lanie White said as she programmed the collars stacked in the bed of a nearby ATV. “It’s expensive, it’s damaging, and it fragments ecosystems.” “Can they outrun an uncertain climate future?” “Wyoming’s 3.5 billion-year-old geologic history reminds us that Earth is ever-changing.” “In response to youth suicides, teachers show students the power of headbanging at Fire in the Mountains festival.”
